- **2026-08-05** — Closed out audit-the-codebase-for-undocumented-business-logic at `app@122f1bd1`, the code-first counterpart to the verify pass: four coverage oracles over all 87 platform pages, two seam indexes over the code, eight classification slices, and a lead-session verification layer that overrode three agent verdicts. **The headline is that the task's own flagship candidate inverted.** Capital flow is indeed the largest undocumented cluster in the codebase — the base cannot name its central nouns, "router" and "dispatch", anywhere outside the repository table — but both its entry points sit behind a flag enabled in `local` only, and its addresses are configured on Sepolia alone. Under this pass's own boundary that makes it an owner question, not documentation work, and it collapsed ten of one slice's twelve claimed absences. Six tasks were filed, ordered corrections-first.

  **Corrected enumerations.** The workspace's own architecture doc is wrong on both counts it states: **9** modules (adds `capitalFlow`, `ens`) and **7** plugins (adds `capitalDistributor`, `lockToVote`, `gaugeVoter`), exactly as the task predicted. Beyond that: **5** boot registries, not 4 — `initActionViewRegistry()` is wired from `providers.tsx` and `layoutRoot.tsx` rather than `initPluginRegistry.ts`, the sharpest instance of the task's "not every registry is wired in the obvious place". **84** dialogs across **19** real registries (24 files match the naming pattern; 4 are form-shape types, 1 an aggregator). **34** slot ids over 6 files, **5** wizard-step registrations, **9** per-account registrations across **7** directories (xmaquina and katana each ship two), **12** feature flags, **55** shared entries.

  **The classification.** *Modules (9):* `governance`, `finance`, `createDao`, `explore`, `dashboard`, `settings`, `application`, `ens` — all covered or yielding narrow absences now filed; `explore` and `ens` fully covered with zero absences. `capitalFlow` — the owner question. *Plugins (7):* `multisig`, `token`, `spp`, `lockToVote` covered by the 28-page governance area, with residual absences in the filed tasks; `admin` ruled out cleanly with none; `capitalDistributor` and `gaugeVoter` covered as capabilities but yielding the integration-depth finding below. *Workspace apps (2):* `apps/app` is the subject; `apps/assistant` plus `assistant-chat` are the support-chat owner question, and `assistant-contracts` is out of scope as shared schema. *Boot registries (5):* plugin slots pre-closed to [plugin slots](../design/plugin-slots.md); the policy registry inside the capital-flow owner question; per-account packs yielding the DAO-slots worked instance; the condition registry ruled out as display machinery, not the catalogue three decisions rejected; the action-view registry covered by [action](../../governance/action.md).

  **A category that spans modules and one that comes from a seam**, both required and both present. Capital flow spans a module, two plugins, three action packs, and the per-account packs across treasury and governance. The onboarding-prompt policy is the seam category: its triggers live in `components/` and `hooks/` rather than `utils/`, and outside the dialog definitions entirely, so two slices each assumed the other held it — it was found only by naming it explicitly.

  **Ships-or-gated, with one qualifier that governs all of it.** 8 of 12 flags omit `production`. The override cookie is honored in production with **no authentication anywhere in its parse path**, so "does not ship" means "off by default", never "unreachable" — every gate note is phrased that way. Two flags are `local`-only: `capitalFlowAutomation`, and `linkedAccount`, which gates a cluster **six pages** describe in the present tense. `permissionsPage` is defined but **never read** — the subpage renders unconditionally everywhere, so [permission model](../../access-control/scoped-authority.md)'s claim holds in production by accident, the gate its own description promises having never been wired. `gateAdvancedGovernance` inverts the usual sense: production-only, and it turns *on* an account-creation cutoff of `2026-04-02` that passed four months ago, set identically in all five env configs and failing closed.

  **Three agent verdicts overridden by first-hand reading.** A claim that code hard-blocks removing an account's last full-execute process, contradicting the owner-validated [removing the last governance process warns, never blocks](../../governance/process-removal.md), was **refuted** — the dialog is an alert with no refusal branch. But the truth is better than either version: uninstall is always routed as a proposal through *another* full-execute process, so removing the last one has no route, not because the product refuses but because the mechanism needs a carrier that would no longer exist. An emergent dead end, not an eligibility guard, and it bears on that page's own open question about recovery paths. Separately, an earlier session note claiming the permissions view was gated out of production was wrong for the reason given above, and the capital-flow slice's absence count was over-collection resolved by checking the gate.

  **Out of scope, argued rather than assumed.** The 70-entry permission-name taxonomy is precisely the catalogue three prior decisions rejected. Observability noise classification is architecture, on the same reasoning that already excluded the backend event catalogue — as is the frontend analytics taxonomy, adjudicated explicitly rather than skipped, and useful only as a cross-check, which it passed. Exit-queue math, multiplier curves, and fee curves stay upstream per [veLocker](../../governance/velocker.md)'s own boundary. Toasts are not a gap at all: the product has no toast mechanism, verified by repo-wide search, and wallet-connection state belongs to a third-party connector.

  **The locale cross-check corroborated the flagship from an unrelated direction.** All 12 `app.*` namespaces have a home in the derived set, so nothing looks invented; exactly one large subtree has no task — `capitalFlow`, at 9.2 KB, larger than `settings` and comparable to `governance` — and it has none because it is dark-launched, not because it was missed.

  **Routing, named not mined:** action-builder composer depth stays with capture-the-action-builder-feature-set; input-sanitization posture across 91 files goes to inventory-the-interaction-principles. The task text reserved wizard per-screen narration for capture-wizard-screen-walkthroughs, which **completed and retired on 2026-08-04** — so that material is covered, not reserved, and the routing note was stale. The four surfaces the verify pass settled were fast-pathed rather than re-mined.

  Three absences were found independently by two slices each — the wizard exit guard, transaction resumption, and a recognized plugin integrating as one stand-alone page with zero governance-slot participation — which is the strongest evidence available that they are real rather than artifacts of one reading. **Board delta:** tasks added — document-governance-derivation-and-availability, document-token-holder-prompts-and-reward-routes, document-interrupted-and-resumed-flows, document-page-and-list-state, document-recognition-and-handoff-limits, document-treasury-composition-and-history (all `ready`); task retired — audit-the-codebase-for-undocumented-business-logic; owner asks added — two rows, one ruling on four built-but-unreachable surfaces and one confirming two behaviors that look unintended; owner asks narrowed — App CMS operating boundary (six fail-open surfaces plus the unauthenticated flag cookie) and token-governance setup (the availability sub-question answered: two gates exist); page questions — one deleted from [reach out when the abstraction cannot stay honest](../../application/getting-help.md#when-the-app-needs-the-aragon-team), taking the platform aggregate to 29 across 22 pages; opportunity added — wire-or-retire-the-permissions-page-gate, the flag that described a restriction it did not impose; no gap opened, closed, or narrowed; one draft-equivalent added as an opportunity entry, and no canonical page changed beyond that one checkbox deletion.

- **2026-08-04** — Closed out verify-documentation-against-source-code, run as four parallel Sonnet passes (one per deliverable) each followed by an adversarial Opus review at `app@122f1bd1`. The reviews mattered: they refuted the pass's flagship invariant finding, overturned a structural claim about slot machinery, and found that a state two pages describe is currently unreachable by its intended route. Everything below is what survived that second pass.

  **Plugin slots — all four halves of the contract, none left implicit.** [Plugin slots](../design/plugin-slots.md) now states what a plugin declares (identity, install version, and the optional setup copy that is what makes it *selectable* in the add-body flow — which is why the admin plugin and the staged proposal processor are installed but never offered as a choice, network availability being a second gate); that skipped slots fall back to a generic default *except* on the transaction path, where a missing registration fails outright, so "required slot" is the exception and the exceptions are the installation ones; that validation is the plugin's own business riding the form it appears in, including cross-field rules like the multisig threshold capped at member count, with no plugin-supplied validation step for the generic flow to call; that transaction composition asks the plugin for the encoded installation call and never inspects it, with update following the same split and uninstall the reverse one (the plugin supplies helper addresses, the generic code encodes around them); and that the definitions live in one registration table each plugin fills from its own folder at startup.

  Two first-pass claims were refuted and are not on the page. Slot fields do **not** all live in one wizard form — the add-body dialog is its own form, which is why a body returns to the designer only once its plugin's fields pass. And DAO slots are **not** separate machinery: a bespoke account registers into the same table keyed by the account instead of the plugin, so [DAO slots](../design/dao-slots.md) now says the two kinds are one mechanism separated by convention, correcting its "isolated in a dedicated area of the codebase" framing to a folder discipline the team upholds rather than a boundary the code enforces. Both citations already on the plugin-slots page were re-verified and are exact.

  **Repository entry points.** [Source repositories](./repositories.md) gains an *Inspect first* column plus a section saying what each entry point owns and what layout fact would otherwise waste a reader's time. Two picks changed under review: `app` resolves to the plugin aggregator rather than the eleven-line bootstrap it feeds, and `app-backend` to the v2 API router rather than the indexer config, which is named as the second stop instead — the API surface is what the product's own areas map onto. Three corrections came with it: the `app` row now notes the monorepo also ships a separately deployed support-chat service; the `app-cms` row's feed description was too narrow and now covers compliance lists and feature flags as well; and a new `aragon-domain` row closes an inconsistency where the base documented that dependency's behavior across four pages without listing it as a source. The page's condition-library question is resolved by the checkout itself and removed. A caveat records that the local `gov-ui-kit` lags the version `app` builds against.

  **The permission boundary holds, with two qualifications.** No grant/revoke dialog, page, or registered action view exists anywhere in the frontend, and the designer's allowlist composer is literally the ordinary action composer with raw calldata excluded — so [permission model](../../access-control/scoped-authority.md)'s "generic account action, not a product feature" framing is correct, and the page now says outright that a composed grant is indistinguishable from any other contract call. What the page under-claimed: permissions *do* have a product surface, a read-only settings view in list and graph form with each condition broken out, which is the derivable-from-chain-state rule in practice rather than an exception to it; and the designer is the *primary* guided flow, not the only one, since admin membership is composed as grants and revocations presented to the user as membership. The empty-allowlist paragraph lost its claim that the app can create that state: the state is real and displayed, but its direct route is broken (filed as an opportunity), and the page now states that no control edits a live allowlist, so such a change is a governed action against the condition contract needing that contract's ABI to render readably. All six specific claims checked held. The allowed-actions list being backend-computed was assessed against the chain-state principle and judged a non-issue — the backend indexes chain events, and the page's independent-verifiability promise is untouched.

  **What the app refuses, and what it batches.** The pass's headline refusal finding did not survive: the support threshold's cap just below 100% is *not* the app being stricter than the protocol. The protocol reference cited to prove it says the opposite — the protocol caps that threshold below 100% itself — so the app is mirroring the protocol's strict-versus-loose comparison, not closing a gap. [Refuse unsatisfiable configuration](../design/invariant-validation.md) therefore presents that cap, the stage threshold bound, and the reused multisig rule as the app *surfacing* protocol bounds, and names exactly one refusal of the app's own: a process must have at least one live way for a proposal to be created. That one is a whole-design check rather than a field bound, it blocks rather than warns, it is test-covered in the app, and it has no protocol counterpart — the contracts would deploy a process nobody can propose into. Three borderline cases were deliberately not published as invariants (a token amount above zero, duplicate permission selectors, and a one-member floor that is emergent from three unrelated UI mechanisms with no rule stating it); the page's checkbox is narrowed to the peripheral setup flows this pass did not open. [Action](../../governance/action.md) now names what arrives as one wallet request — any multi-action proposal, direct execution, the designer's two-signature install whose proposal can carry a dozen-plus granular actions, and uninstall or update — plus the case where the publishing process is the bootstrap admin plugin and creation *is* execution, so one signature performs the whole bundle atomically. Its checkbox is narrowed to the wallet half, which is now provably beyond the source's reach: the app hands the wallet only a target, a value, and calldata, with no typed data, batching capability, or human-readable hint, so the confirmation dialog is entirely the wallet's own decoding.

  **Filed rather than absorbed.** Two product opportunities: publish-a-process-with-no-selected-actions (the designer's terminal step fails silently when the author never opened the actions list, affecting both radio choices — established by library-level reproduction rather than a browser run, and flagged as such in the entry) and pre-empt-protocol-rejected-configuration (three advanced-flow values the app accepts and the chain refuses: a zero stage expiration that breaks two strict inequalities, a duplicate body address in one stage where the error plumbing exists but is never populated, and no upper bound on a voting duration). One page question added to [Governance designer](../../governance/governance-designer.md): whether the absolute floor on approving voting power should stay unexposed, since both token-based paths install it as zero with an explicit code comment saying so. Two findings were recorded without filing: the product workspace's hand-written structure and slot docs have drifted from the code, now a caution on the repositories page and in the audit task's *Where to look*; and the backend's vendored contract bindings demonstrably do not track the published artifacts packages, since it declares no dependency on them — no product-semantic consequence, so it stays here. The audit task's three forward references to this pass were updated from pending to settled. **Board delta:** task retired — verify-documentation-against-source-code (row removed, file deleted); opportunities added — publish-a-process-with-no-selected-actions, pre-empt-protocol-rejected-configuration (both `candidate`, governance); owner ask extended — the token-governance row gains a fourth call; page question added — Governance designer (approving-power floor); page question resolved and removed — Source repositories (condition library public); no knowledge gap opened, closed, or narrowed; drafts unchanged at 74; open questions 30 across 23 platform pages.

- **2026-08-04** — Mined the product owner's app-metadata-input briefing and closed the backlog's top knowledge gap. - **2026-08-04** — Closed out derive-the-executor-acl-auth-model's remaining agent-owned portion, run as three parallel Sonnet passes (OSx-route validation, one-pager disposition, rejected-terminology sweep) followed by one adversarial Opus review, which caught a fabricated execution route the first pass had introduced. **Routes and callers.** [OSx authorization mapping](../../access-control/osx-authorization-paths.md) now leads its alternate-route section with the rule that the plugin's route decides which address action targets see, then names three: the default `Call` on the plugin's own DAO; `DelegateCall` to the shared GlobalExecutor, where the loop runs in the plugin's context so targets see the plugin; and `Call` to another executor, which must expose the same `execute` entry point, makes the action calls itself, and is therefore the caller its targets see. That third route carries the consequence the page was missing: another DAO applies its own `EXECUTE_PERMISSION_ID` check, while the standalone executor applies none, so a target authorizing the shared instance has authorized anyone who can reach it. Nested execution and the reentrancy rule are linked upstream rather than restated. The ROOT paragraph stopped re-enumerating dao.md's end states and states the product consequence instead: ROOT is not a runtime shortcut past a guard, but a ROOT holder can grant itself execute and act as the account, so ROOT is total control over the organization's authority.

  **A wrong route was found and removed, on two pages.** The first validation pass proposed, and the mapping page briefly asserted, that a staged proposal processor can be a `Call` route target holding `EXECUTE_PERMISSION_ID` on its DAO. That is impossible: the processor's `execute` takes a proposal ID, not the executor interface's `(callId, actions, allowFailureMap)` — confirmed in `app-backend`'s `stagedProposalProcessor` artifact against `osx` `Plugin.sol`'s `IExecutor(_target).execute(...)` — so the selectors cannot match. The claim was inherited from [target](../../governance/target.md) and had propagated: target, [governance designer](../../governance/governance-designer.md), [process](../../governance/process.md), [body](../../governance/body.md), and the governance index all now say that the processor is the *action's* target rather than the plugin's route, and that what a plugin's approved actions accomplish — not its `TargetConfig.target` — is what makes it a process or a body. The app gives each plugin body it installs a `DelegateCall` route through the shared GlobalExecutor (`app` `pluginTransactionUtils.getPluginTargetConfig`), so the body's actions run in its own context and its `reportProposalResult` call arrives from the body's own address, which is the only kind of report the processor counts; a body that is not a plugin has no route to configure. **Verification scope:** `osx` `Plugin.sol`, `Executor.sol`, `DAO.sol`, `PermissionManager.sol`, `DaoAuthorizable.sol`/`auth.sol`, the pinned dao/permissions/execution/auth/plugins pages, and `app`/`app-backend` for the target-config and processor facts. The staged processor's own Solidity is not checked out locally, so any processor-side claim rests on protocol-doc plus those app artifacts. Everything else held as written: the `dao.hasPermission(plugin, caller, permissionId, calldata)` argument order, the DAO's own-storage `auth` resolution, the `EXECUTE_PERMISSION_ID` gate with calldata reaching the condition, the ROOT claims, the governance-replacement claims, and the grant-event and silent-no-op signal claims.

  **One-pager disposition.** `inbox/acl-one-pager.md` is mined and removed. Most of its positioning material was already covered, in several places more carefully than the source stated it. Three claims did not survive validation and are carried nowhere: "targets trust only the executor" (exclusivity is the target's own configuration), "a single contained permission update" (the mapping page already says not necessarily one), and "introduced or retired by simply granting/revoking permissions" (adding a body is a plugin installation, not only a permission change). Three claims lacked homes and got them, all in the product's register rather than the source's: [permission model](../../access-control/scoped-authority.md) now carries the source's central argument — authority written into each target contract means a governance change has to be repeated, sequenced, and re-reviewed on every one of them, growing with the contract count and leaving one group's authority fragmented rather than expressed once — plus the integration starting points (transfer `Ownable` ownership, grant the roles and revoke legacy holders, or name the account at deployment) and the correction that sole authorization is the target's configuration, not something the account asserts. [Value proposition](../../value-proposition.md)'s evolving-governance bullet gained the customer-facing form: governance can be replaced without reconfiguring the contracts the organization already governs. The source's Path A/Path B framing, "governor-executor monolith", "blast radius", "boundary", and "governance legos" are deliberately not carried; the mechanisms behind Paths A and B already live on safe-vs-aragon-multisig, stages-over-direct-permissions, and create-transaction. The "new app / `onlyOwner` / RBAC" checklist was not promoted to a guide — the enabling capability is documented and the app has no flow behind it — and guide-portfolio decisions belong to rethink-the-guide-layer.

  **Terminology sweep: clean.** This was a scan for the rejected vocabulary, not a correctness audit of every page. Every canonical page in the root, `accounts/`, `governance/`, `treasury/`, `access-control/`, `design/`, and `guides/` was read, plus both model pages' backlinks and the concurrent optimistic-governance page. No page uses runtime-principal, execution-engine, authority-path, policy-as-authorizer, boundary-as-enforcement-point, direct-authorization, effective-control, disembodied-governor, or loose-ACL framing in the rejected sense; every keyword hit was ordinary English. The route error above was a factual defect the vocabulary sweep was not looking for, which is why the adversarial review caught it and the sweep did not. The two access-control pages stay `status: draft` for the separate drafts review. **Board delta:** task retired — derive-the-executor-acl-auth-model (row removed, file deleted); verify-documentation-against-source-code is now the first *Ready to run* row; no owner ask added or resolved; no gap opened, closed, or narrowed; no draft added or removed.

- **2026-08-04** — Mined the product owner's optimistic-governance briefing and closed the backlog's top knowledge gap. New draft [Optimistic governance](../../governance/optimistic-governance.md) gives the concept one product-level home: it is an accept-unless-challenged decision pattern rather than one implementation; it reduces routine affirmative-voting, quorum, and transaction overhead by letting designated proposers author the concrete proposal while veto holders still monitor for proposals that need an objection; and its calendar duration still includes a protected objection window. The page distinguishes the general pattern from the platform's precise staged expression — vetoing bodies, no approval requirement, a positive veto threshold, and an explicit advance call after the window — and makes the institutional ordering configurable. The owner-described recurring core-team-or-multisig → token-holder-veto pattern and the community-approval → security-council-veto variant are examples, not prescribed bodies or stage order.

  The same page records the central failure mode: default passage is only protective when veto holders discover, understand, and can act on a proposal before the window closes. [Multisig gates](../../governance/multisig-gates.md), [Stage](../../governance/stage.md), [Staged proposals](../../governance/proposal.md#staged-proposals), [Gradual permission handover](../../access-control/gradual-permission-handover.md), [Value proposition](../../value-proposition.md), the [multisig guide](../../guides/multisigs-in-advanced-governance.md), and the governance index now link to that definition instead of partially re-defining it. The multisig pages also separate post-token approval from post-token veto rather than assigning security councils one universal role. The owner characterized Aragon's old Govern product, paired with Aragon Court as its escalation mechanism, as a pioneering implementation championed by Jorge Izquierdo; that remains attributed historical provenance rather than present-tense product behavior. Arbitration, optimistic-oracle, and optimistic-rollup analogies likewise remain context rather than importing those mechanisms into the canonical governance model.

  The guardian-DAO guide candidate on rethink-the-guide-layer now preserves the concrete use-case sketch: a vault curator or operator proposes changes while liquidity providers or another token-holder constituency can veto. It also preserves the owner's warning that this is only a sketch and the guide needs its own deeper treatment; no premature guide or Guardian DAO concept was created. The draft inventory rises from 72 to 73; the open-question, task, and opportunity inventories are otherwise unchanged. **Board delta:** knowledge gap closed and owner ask removed — optimistic governance; draft added — Optimistic governance; existing blocked task rethink-the-guide-layer refined without a state or ordering change; no task or opportunity added or retired.

- **2026-08-04** — Rebuilt the authorization and execution model from first principles after the owner's line-by-line review rejected the draft's generic authority stack. Two Sol producer audits split the work into governor/social-choice semantics and concrete authorization/call execution; an independent Terra review then attacked the integrated rewrite. Primary-source checks against OpenZeppelin Governor, Ownable, AccessControl, and AccessManager and the pinned OSx DAO, permissions, execution, auth, and plugin pages supported the correction. The prior draft had confused a governor with its voting method, denied governors could be addressable entities, treated timelocks/oracle thresholds/keepers as possible governors, invented a fixed two-boundary runtime-principal → policy → engine path, and used ACL as a synonym for access control.

  [Authorization and execution model](../../access-control/authorization-and-execution.md) now starts from how actors' preferences and desires become onchain state transitions: a governor entity resolves proposed actions using a decision method; each EVM call has a concrete caller; and each guarded function accepts or rejects that caller. It gives explicit caller-preserving flows for OpenZeppelin Governor with and without a timelock, Safe as a governor–account combination with multisignature governance baked into the account contract, and the normal Aragon plugin → DAO → target path. The page removes action plans, runtime principals, resources/capabilities, enforcement points, authorizer/policy, policy administrators, execution engines, direct authorization, effective control, and the claim of exactly two authorization boundaries. ACL now means a list-like permission store: OSx `PermissionManager` and OpenZeppelin `AccessManager` are ACL-style; scattered `Ownable` fields are access control but not one ACL. All five former page questions are answered and removed.

  [OSx authorization mapping](../../access-control/osx-authorization-paths.md) was rebuilt as concrete call tables and flows, including guarded plugin functions, ROOT-gated permission administration, Safe calls, and `DelegateCall` through GlobalExecutor where action targets see the plugin rather than the DAO. The Terra review caught and corrected residual absolutes in [Account](../../accounts/account.md), [Safe vs Aragon multisig](../../guides/safe-vs-aragon-multisig.md), [Stages over direct permission grants](../../governance/multisig-gates.md#stages-over-direct-permission-grants), and [Target](../../governance/target.md); it also caught the Body/Safe contradiction, so [Body](../../governance/body.md) now describes who supplies preferences in a given process rather than asserting that a body can never create or act, and its stale confirmation question is removed. [Process](../../governance/process.md), [Plugin](../../governance/plugin.md), [Action](../../governance/action.md), [Permission model](../../access-control/scoped-authority.md), and the linked-account pages were reconciled to the same caller-grounded model; permission-signal wording now respects idempotent grants that emit no event. Historical log entries remain unchanged; the active task Progress explicitly marks the rejected interpretation superseded. The one-pager capture remains because its positioning/integration claims still require a separate source-disposition subtask.

  A second top-to-bottom owner review tightened the core page without adding another ontology. Two Terra passes checked the OpenZeppelin account/timelock facts and rebuilt the caller argument around a transaction's call trace; two Sol passes then attacked the integrated text for factual and editorial regressions. The result removes universal governor recordkeeping, scopes the Safe example to its basic owner-threshold path, identifies the TimelockController as the account in `GovernorTimelockControl`, distinguishes guards from their local or shared configuration, and explains intermediate guards through the consequential state changes they can enable. The standalone authorized-intermediary, permission-administration, ACL-correction, and stable-account sections were removed; ACL is now defined where shared configuration is introduced, and the stable-DAO consequence remains only in the OSx mapping.

  A third owner pass reordered the page around its reader prerequisites. Consequential state changes now establish why the model matters before the page defines actions, targets, callers, and `msg.sender`; only then does it distinguish governors from accounts and follow execution through a nested call trace. The detailed TimelockController behavior moved beside its trace, and the authorization section now states the central distinction directly: every guard remains on a target function, while the configuration it consults may be local to that target or shared through an authority or DAO permission store. The call-trace close and authorization close both return to the consequential effects introduced at the top.

  A fourth owner pass added the transaction origin and security motivation that connects those concepts. The page now establishes that ordinary Ethereum transactions begin with an EOA, contract accounts call only after execution enters them, and a multi-transaction governance lifecycle therefore consists of several EOA-rooted traces. It contrasts a plain EOA's signing-key control with the richer thresholds, recovery, delays, and scoped rules a smart contract account can enforce, then introduces the governor as the component deciding which actions that account should take. The execution diagrams now show their EOA roots. The authorization close also makes its two classifications orthogonal: local/shared describes where authorization configuration or logic lives, while ACL describes one representation of permissions; OSx `PermissionManager` is both shared and a direct ACL, whereas OpenZeppelin `AccessManager` is shared and role-based. **Board delta:** owner ask resolved and removed — authority model; task derive-the-executor-acl-auth-model remains `in-progress` and moved to the top of *Ready to run* for its bounded OSx-route validation and one-pager disposition; six page questions resolved — the five authorization-model questions plus Body's absolute-prohibition confirmation; no task, opportunity, or knowledge gap added or retired.

  A fifth owner pass tightened the account-to-governance transition. The account section no longer introduces the defined term governance process early or detours into relaying, account abstraction, recovery, modules, and other mechanisms. It now moves directly from EOA-rooted transactions, through the plain EOA's signing-key decision rule, to governed smart-contract execution and the governor → account → guarded target flow. **Board delta:** none.

  A sixth owner pass rebuilt the authorization close around concrete consequences. The intermediate-function rationale now sits beside the guard definition and explains why an unguarded `DAO.execute` would let any caller direct the DAO's assets and permissions, while an unguarded delegated plugin entry point would expose the delegated action to anyone. The ACL discussion is reduced to one distinction—shared describes a common authorization source, while ACL describes explicit permission entries—and the custom-authority and permission-model detours are gone. **Board delta:** none.

  A seventh owner pass clarified task ownership rather than changing the semantic model. Derive-the-executor-acl-auth-model and its board row now state at the top that the owner's material is complete and that every remaining item is agent-owned; final approval of the draft page remains in the separate drafts inventory. **Board delta:** wording clarified on the existing Ready-to-run row; no state or ordering change.

- **2026-08-04** — Commissioned the missing code-first direction. The owner judged verify-documentation-against-source-code sound but one-directional: it corrects four named pages against the code and, because all three of its load-bearing sections name those same four page-sets, discovery is bounded by pages that already exist. Its "record any additional finding" bullet is a disposal rule, not a search instruction — a session that opens only the files the four traces require and files nothing is fully compliant. That task keeps its scope (widening it would restore the ambiguity its 2026-08-04 reframing removed) and gains one scope-boundary bullet routing discovery elsewhere. New ready task **audit-the-codebase-for-undocumented-business-logic** carries the other direction: carve the app into business-logic categories, classify each against the existing area map, and convert absences into at most six finite tasks. The justification recorded in the entry is that every completeness instrument this base owns is self-referential — `unresolved` sees only promises already made, `orphans` only pages already written, and a knowledge gap only a hole someone already noticed — so nothing asks the code what the product does that no page mentions.

  Three design corrections are baked into the entry rather than left to the running session. The deliverable is a coverage classification against the five existing areas, not a second taxonomy competing with them; it is pinned to `app` `122f1bd1` so exhaustion is decidable and a later audit is a fresh task at a fresh commit; and the artifact deliberately does not become durable — the classification lives in the close-out log and the spawned tasks, because a canonical coverage page would fail the present-tense product-truth contract, a backlog inventory row would be the hand-maintained second copy that section bans, and an `inbox/` capture is reserved for non-regenerable owner material. Four checkable constraints guard the dominant failure mode, which is transcribing the sixteen module and plugin directory names as categories: a name test (no path, module, component, hook, or route identifier in any category name), a one-sentence shape test, a hard no-canonical-page-edit rule, and a production-reachability rule so gated or dark-launched code becomes an owner question rather than a documentation task. The task-count cap cites the thirty-to-twenty-two consolidation, and the entry states outright that a low task count is success.

  Scope was cut against four siblings rather than merged: no page intersection with verify-documentation-against-source-code (the audit writes no canonical page), cross-surface recurring rules stay with the owner-blocked inventory-the-interaction-principles, and wizard and action-builder depth stay reserved for owner material. Two stale forward references were repaired — capture-wizard-screen-walkthroughs and rethink-the-guide-layer both deferred work to the retired ground-the-base-in-the-source-repos slug, and now name verify-documentation-against-source-code and the new audit respectively. Three verified leads seed the pass: `apps/assistant` is a support-chat intake service in the same monorepo that no page in this base mentions and whose product scope is an owner call; [reach out when the abstraction cannot stay honest](../../application/getting-help.md#when-the-app-needs-the-aragon-team)'s handoff-inventory checkbox is code-answerable and therefore a task masquerading as an owner question; and the capital-flow policy, router, and dispatch surface has no page at all.

  Four agent passes ran — a code-first survey of `app`, a documentation coverage map, an adversarial task-design critique, and an adversarial verification of the written entry. The verification caught the pass's own overreach and it was corrected: the draft had claimed the four-step transaction lifecycle, its resume and duplicate-submission guard, control gating, and alert severity as undocumented, when [transaction submission](../../application/submitting-a-transaction.md), [control availability](../design/control-availability.md), and [alert severity](../design/alert-severity.md) already own all four — the entry now names them as covered so the running session cannot repeat the error, and the design characterization was narrowed from "one arc" to "its strongest cluster." It also found three of the four guard rules had no Done-when test; the criteria now check the name and sentence tests by inspection, require reachability marks, require that the category set not be the module tree, and require a zero-absence result to be argued rather than silent, replacing an `raw/`-empty criterion that an untouched scratch directory satisfied trivially. [Reach out when the abstraction cannot stay honest](../../application/getting-help.md#when-the-app-needs-the-aragon-team) left the settle-first draft batch, since a queued row now touches it. A third stale slug was repaired in capture-wizard-screen-walkthroughs, which still routed dialog-wizard shape to the retired grounding task rather than to the settled [dialog taxonomy](../design/dialog-taxonomy.md). No code was changed and no canonical page was edited.

  Both entries were then pulled back to nudge altitude on the owner's direction: the product owner sets the intent and the guard rails, not the procedure. The audit lost its glob catalogue, its per-file path enumerations, and its step-by-step method, keeping *Where to look* as the seams worth following — registries and slot definitions, dialog definitions, the reachability gates, the translation file as an independent partition — plus the two traps and the split-repo warning. Verify lost its per-repository depth instructions and its field-by-field slot prescription in favor of naming the four outcomes. Every load-bearing decision survived in both: the commit pin, the six-task cap, the low-count-is-success framing, the implementation-independent naming rule, the product-behavior test, no canonical page edits, and the routing to the four sibling tasks. **Board delta:** task added — audit-the-codebase-for-undocumented-business-logic (`ready`, second in *Ready to run*); verify-documentation-against-source-code gained a scope boundary and stays `ready` and first; two retired-slug references repaired; no owner ask, opportunity, or knowledge gap added or closed.

- **2026-08-04** — Resolved the first Ready-for-input item from the owner's scope answer. - **2026-08-04** — Completed mine-platform-infrastructure-and-general-ui by reconciling the owner's final two ordered release-note categories against the current app (`122f1bd1`), app-backend (`107103b`), and pinned protocol documentation (`2d800ee`).   New draft [Contract upgrades](../../accounts/contract-upgrades.md) gives the settings capability one product-level home: when enabled for an eligible account and an update is available, the app can package OSx DAO and compatible in-place plugin upgrades into a proposal through a selected governance process. It links the pinned DAO and plugin mechanisms rather than restating them, and preserves the owner's deliberate opt-in posture: executable-code changes carry risk, many releases only add features an account does not need, and the app does not push every update as mandatory maintenance. - **2026-08-04** — Completed mine-gauge-voting-release-notes by reconciling releases 1.15, 1.16, the editorially normalized 1.21, 1.25, and 1.27 in the ordered **Gauge voting** category against the current app (`122f1bd1`), app-backend (`107103b`), and public `aragon/ve-governance` contracts (`develop@408ad144`). New draft [Gauge voting](../../governance/gauge-voting.md) defines the Gauge Voter as a non-proposal, generally signaling governance capability: eligible holders allocate voting power by relative weight across active gauge addresses during an epoch voting window, while the plugin itself neither creates `IProposal` objects nor executes DAO actions. The UI normalizes nonzero weights into two-decimal percentages totaling 100%, and the contract replaces the voter's prior allocation in its current write state. Epoch behavior is configuration-dependent rather than the release note's unresolved “roll over or not” shorthand: update-hook deployments use persistent epoch-zero allocation and can recalculate downward as power falls, while non-hook deployments use the current epoch and an epoch-start voting-power snapshot.

- **2026-08-04** — Completed mine-aragon-profiles-brief as a claim-by-claim reconciliation of the owner's pre-launch Profiles marketing brief and the ordered **Member identity: Aragon Names, ENS profiles & delegate statements** release-note category against the current app (`122f1bd1`), app-backend (`107103b`), and pinned protocol documentation (`2d800ee`). The narrow [Aragon Names](../../application/aragon-names.md) seed is now the overview of four separately owned drafts: [ENS as the profile layer](../../application/aragon-profiles.md#ens-as-the-profile-layer) records the decision to keep ENS authoritative rather than create a proprietary profile database; [Claiming an Aragon Name](../../application/aragon-names.md#claiming) owns eligibility, mainnet transactions, constraints, rename, and release; [Aragon Profiles](../../application/aragon-profiles.md) owns resolution, display, editing, and member-profile context; and [Delegate profile record](../../governance/delegate-profile-record.md) owns the network-and-token ENS key, IPFS pointer, and current editor document shape. The decision page explicitly corrects the release note's “everything on blockchain” shorthand: ENS stores the records and pointer on mainnet, while statement JSON and app-pinned avatar files live on IPFS; backend retrieval, caching, indexing, and pinning are delivery infrastructure rather than a competing profile authority.

  **Every Done/ETA claim received a current-source disposition.** Mainnet ENS name, avatar, and text-record reads are centralized in the app's ENS module and reused across identity surfaces. The member page displays the primary name, avatar, description, and links, and can additionally show delegation stats, a token-specific statement, and an optional EFP follower/following card when their respective conditions are met. The feature-flagged profile dialogs edit the supported ENS fields with signed mainnet resolver writes and pin a newly uploaded avatar first. Profile onboarding while viewing a DAO and the user menu offer creation to a wallet without a primary ENS; the claim flow reads `isRegistered` and `memberSubdomain`, calls the fixed-address Member Registry's `register`, then calls the mainnet reverse registrar to set the new primary name. Both requests send zero native value, while the wallet still pays gas. The current frontend is therefore wired to a mainnet registry deployment and a self-service claim call, but local source does not establish that deployment's audit status, current `aragon.eth` claim authority, or eviction/upgrade permission holders; that uncertainty is preserved on the claiming page and promoted on the board instead of being asserted.

  **The brief's stale rename/release status was resolved in favor of current code, not either prose source.** Rename is a live two-transaction flow: the app enumerates indexed text records, reads the resolver's live address and contenthash, supplies that supported record tuple to `MemberRegistry.move`, and then sets the new name as primary. The page deliberately limits the persistence promise to those record types rather than “every ENS record.” Release is also live for a primary `.aragon.eth` name: the confirmation flow calls `MemberRegistry.release` on mainnet, and the upstream clean-handover mechanism clears the resolver state before the label is reusable. Because both app flows exist, no rename or release opportunity was created; the task's later unconditional opportunity wording was treated as superseded by its own evidence-first conflict rule.

  Delegate statements are also implemented end to end. The app builds `<EIP-3770-shortname>.<lowercase-token-address>.delegate` (Sepolia uses `test`), writes an `ipfs://` CID to the primary ENS resolver on mainnet, and its editor pins version-1 Markdown JSON with `version`, `type`, `format`, and `content`. The backend reader accepts nonempty string content and normalizes it rather than enforcing the entire writer schema; the member page renders that content and offers create/edit controls to the connected member address. Whether ENS endorses the convention remains the second promoted owner question. Release-only facts also landed: the delegation stats card's voting power, token balance, and received-delegation count; conditional EFP API context; and CMS-selected featured addresses replacing the onboarded dashboard's ordinary member section and becoming the first/default Members tab when a Token Voting body matches. - **2026-08-04** — Folded the owner's authority-vocabulary answer into the base, advancing derive-the-executor-acl-auth-model without closing it. **Two nouns are settled.** A **governor** is a decision *process*, not an entity: the rule that aggregates the distinct preferences of multiple actors into one choice (the owner's own framing, by analogy to social choice theory). It has no address and is not a party to any call, so "which contract is the governor?" is a category error; in this product it appears as a [body](../../governance/body.md) of actors plus the rule aggregating them, composed and carried to execution by a [governance process](../../governance/process.md). An **account** is an address that can act — defined by exposing a generic execution entry point that takes a target, value, and calldata and makes that call as itself — with externally-owned accounts (the keyholder can sign anything) and smart contract accounts (an Aragon account's `execute`, a Safe's equivalent) as its two kinds. "Executor" is therefore not a second role: an account *is* an executor, and upstream's own name for that hat ("the executor" on [DAO](../../protocol-doc/core/dao.md)) is recorded as the protocol layer's wording rather than a competing product term. The owner explicitly **rejected** the protocol-wide reading in which every address is an account — an ERC-20 address is not one — so [account](../../accounts/account.md) states the narrower capability-based sense and notes the broader Ethereum-docs usage only so protocol material stays readable; the Ethereum account model is cited, not restated.

  Reconciled every page that carried the older vocabulary as load-bearing prose, rewriting to the new present rather than narrating the change: [account](../../accounts/account.md) gained the definition and an explicit *an account is not a governor* section; [safe vs Aragon multisig](../../guides/safe-vs-aragon-multisig.md) now describes a coupled **governor–account monolith** whose authorization rule ships in the same contract as the execution entry point it authorizes (rigid in that changing who decides means changing the thing that acts — not that Safe cannot be extended); [authorization and execution model](../../access-control/authorization-and-execution.md) renamed its *decision mechanism* and *execution identity* roles to governor and account and now links the account definition instead of duplicating it; [OSx authorization mapping](../../access-control/osx-authorization-paths.md) renamed the same two rows and its topology section; and [stages over direct permission grants](../../governance/multisig-gates.md#stages-over-direct-permission-grants), [target](../../governance/target.md), [vault](../../treasury/vault.md), the [treasury index](../../treasury/index.md), and [transactions](../../treasury/transactions.md) lost "arbitrary executor" as a competing noun. All the pages that were waiting now link the model — [target](../../governance/target.md) on the two authorization boundaries, [linked-account signaling](../../accounts/linked-account.md#establishing-the-relationship) on grants that sit outside the authority path entirely, [linking does not imply control](../../accounts/linked-account.md#linking-does-not-imply-control) on direct authorization versus effective control — so `wiki backlinks` shows the dependency rather than prose restating it.

  Two of the model page's six open questions are answered and deleted; a new residual replaced them (can a decision rule with no actor preferences to aggregate — a time lock, an oracle threshold, a keeper — be a governor?), and the four untouched ones stand: policy administration, target-side authorization as a formal second boundary, effective control as a first-class concept, and what **ACL** names. mine-permissions-and-access-control's vocabulary constraint was updated accordingly — governor and account are now *required* usage, only "ACL" stays hands-off — which removes the ordering hazard that had it writing around three unsettled labels. Composite gate run. **Board delta:** owner ask resolved in part — the authority-vocabulary input row is replaced by a narrower row 3 covering only the remaining boundary and naming questions, and the optimistic-governance briefing moves to input row 1 as the ask that now blocks most (it gates the first row of *Ready to run*); task refined — derive-the-executor-acl-auth-model (still in progress, Work pruned to the open questions, Progress recorded, a new Done-when criterion added: no page presents "executor" as an entity distinct from an account) and mine-permissions-and-access-control; no gap opened or closed; drafts inventory unchanged.

Every row states why it is there, what it produces, and the surfaces it affects; blocked rows name their blocker; and position is the only priority signal, so the "band the queue Now/Next/Later" rule and the per-task `Impact:` ratings both retired — the ratings reworded to "Where the value is likely to be" rather than deleted, so their substance survived. The one highlight and all five knowledge gaps became input rows, so everything waiting on the owner now reads from one section, while the drafts list, the `wiki checkboxes` aggregate, and `unresolved` moved to Inventories.   **Queue consolidation: 30 open tasks → 22, plus this one retired.** Six merges, each because the tasks had been split by *source* where the work is one *outcome*, and each carrying over every claim, pointer, and scope boundary from its inputs: **mine-permissions-and-access-control** (← mine-granular-access-control-brief + mine-permissions-release-notes), **mine-token-governance-setup** (← mine-lock-to-vote-brief + mine-lock-to-vote-release-notes + mine-token-voting-erc20-release-notes), **mine-velocker** (← mine-velocker-brief + mine-velocker-release-notes), **mine-proposal-lifecycle-and-actions** (← mine-proposal-lifecycle-release-notes + mine-action-builder-release-notes), **mine-admin-and-dao-presentation** (← mine-dao-creation-admin-release-notes + catalogue-admin-plugin-differences), and **mine-platform-infrastructure-and-general-ui** (← mine-platform-infrastructure-release-notes + mine-general-ui-release-notes). The token-governance merge changes an outcome rather than merely saving a pass: the LockToVote brief's selection rule ("a vanilla ERC-20 takes lock to vote") is incomplete without the release notes' automatic-wrapper branch, so a decision page written from the brief alone would have been wrong. Three tasks were deliberately *not* merged despite sharing a page — capture-the-action-builder-feature-set (blocked on owner material, and it owns a different section of proposal-actions than the release notes' action-list controls; the boundary is now stated on both), plus the page-specific Voting Terminal and dialog-taxonomy groundings, which stay independent of the owner-blocked broad codebase pass.   **Ordering rewritten on rework-avoided rather than on value**, and the rule recorded in WORKFLOW: vocabulary, shared mechanisms, and area framing before the work that would re-invent or contradict them; product semantics before the presentation layer that renders them; cheap passes several later rows depend on early; scattered small-fact passes last, once their homes exist. The concrete calls: governance-model reconciliation leads because it carries a terminology ruling (the owner rejects "multisig gating"/"safe gating" for optimistic governance) against the most-depended-on pages in the base — governance-designer alone has 65 backlinks; the transaction-dialog stepper and the two-step deploy-then-propose pattern move to row 2 so three later rows cite one home instead of each re-explaining it; app-cms moves to row 3 as the hub three later rows link; aragon-profiles precedes the token panel and the DAO's own ENS name because it re-scopes `accounts/` to admit participant identity; premium/partially-supported services precedes its three plugin instances so none invents the phrasing; the Voting Terminal grounding follows both the corrected per-body role model and the gauge decision it must reflect. Shared facts got single owners where several passes each claimed one: featured delegates (CMS mechanism vs delegate surface vs delegation), the DAO's `dao.eth` name vs the participant's `aragon.eth`, the profile's summary cards vs the token panel, and the wallet retry-safety warning vs the submission stepper.

  **WORKFLOW.md** now carries the model: the four sections plus the one-item-one-row-one-section and position-is-the-priority rules; the merge rule (one task per surface-and-source-set, with "check whether the sibling task already ran" named as the smell of a merge that should have happened); the row contract and how a section mirrors an entry's `status`; the ordering rule; and a reconciliation step rewritten for sections rather than state labels. "Queue row" was normalized to "board row" across every entry.
